Finally, it can raise awareness among researchers and practitioners about the potential unintended consequences of alignment procedures.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**
The paper presents a novel approach to a clearly defined problem, provides empirical evidence linking LHF to lexical preferences, and raises important ethical considerations. However, the limited scope of the study (single model family, scientific abstracts in English) and the relatively small effect size slightly detract from its overall impact. It is a solid piece of work with significant potential to influence future research on alignment and bias in LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Agent-Based Feature Generation from Clinical Notes for Outcome Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01956v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayi Wang, Jacqueline Jil Vallon, Neil Panjwani, Xi Ling, Sushmita Vij, Sandy Srinivas, John Leppert, Mark K. Buyyounouski, Mohsen Bayati
- **Abstract**: Electronic health records (EHRs) contain rich unstructured clinical notes that could enhance predictive modeling, yet extracting meaningful features from these notes remains challenging. Current approaches range from labor-intensive manual clinician feature generation (CFG) to fully automated representational feature generation (RFG) that lack interpretability and clinical relevance. Here we introduce SNOW (Scalable Note-to-Outcome Workflow), a modular multi-agent system powered by large language models (LLMs) that autonomously generates structured clinical features from unstructured notes without human intervention. We evaluated SNOW against manual CFG, clinician-guided LLM approaches, and RFG methods for predicting 5-year prostate cancer recurrence in 147 patients from Stanford Healthcare. While manual CFG achieved the highest performance (AUC-ROC: 0.771), SNOW matched this performance (0.761) without requiring any clinical expertise, significantly outperforming both baseline features alone (0.691) and all RFG approaches. The clinician-guided LLM method also performed well (0.732) but still required expert input. SNOW's specialized agents handle feature discovery, extraction, validation, post-processing, and aggregation, creating interpretable features that capture complex clinical information typically accessible only through manual review. Our findings demonstrate that autonomous LLM systems can replicate expert-level feature engineering at scale, potentially transforming how clinical ML models leverage unstructured EHR data while maintaining the interpretability essential for clinical deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SNOW (Scalable Note-to-Outcome Workflow), a novel agent-based system powered by large language models (LLMs) designed to autonomously generate structured clinical features from unstructured notes.  The system aims to bridge the gap between labor-intensive manual clinician feature generation (CFG) and less interpretable, fully automated representational feature generation (RFG). SNOW is evaluated on the task of predicting 5-year prostate cancer recurrence using EHR data, and the results demonstrate that SNOW matches the performance of manual CFG without requiring clinical expertise, while significantly outperforming baseline features and RFG approaches. The paper highlights the potential of autonomous LLM systems to replicate expert-level feature engineering at scale, maintaining interpretability essential for clinical deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in its *autonomous* approach to clinical feature engineering. While LLMs have been used in healthcare before, this paper distinguishes itself by creating a multi-agent system that handles the *entire* feature generation pipeline—discovery, extraction, validation, post-processing, and aggregation—without requiring any explicit human prompt engineering or expert specification of variables. SNOW's modular architecture, mimicking clinical reasoning, further contributes to its novelty. The comparison to prior methods which require expert prompts is a key differentiator.
*   **Significance:** The potential significance of SNOW is substantial. The ability to automate feature engineering from unstructured clinical data at the level of expert clinicians would significantly reduce the reliance on manual abstraction, potentially transforming clinical ML model development. This could lead to more rapid development and deployment of personalized AI-driven healthcare solutions. The focus on interpretability is critical for clinical adoption.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical evaluation, with a well-defined patient cohort and comparison against multiple baselines (CFG, RFG, clinician-guided LLMs, baseline features).
    *   Clear description of the SNOW architecture and agent responsibilities.
    *   Emphasis on interpretability and clinical relevance of generated features.
    *   Demonstrated capability to match the performance of manual expert feature engineering.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is limited to a single clinical task (5-year prostate cancer recurrence prediction) and a specific dataset. Generalizability to other tasks and data sources needs to be explored.
    *   While it matches CFG performance, it only does so within the confines of nested cross validation. Its long term, real-world impact has not been investigated.
    *   The paper provides relatively little detail on the specific prompts and LLM configurations used within each agent. This makes it difficult to replicate and potentially limits the ability to adapt the system to other domains.
    *   The computational cost and resource requirements of the SNOW system are not discussed in detail. Running LLMs can be resource intensive.

*   **Impact:** The impact of the paper could be high if the system proves to be generalizable and scalable. It opens up new avenues for leveraging unstructured clinical data in ML models and could potentially democratize access to high-quality features. Further research is needed to address the weaknesses and demonstrate broader applicability.

*   **Score Justification:**

Given the novelty of its autonomous approach, its solid evaluation methodology, and the potentially high impact in transforming clinical ML model development, a score of 8 is justified. This reflects the promising aspects of the paper, acknowledging the limitations concerning generalizability and practical deployability that still need further investigation. The system's potential to automate expert-level feature engineering while prioritizing interpretability represents a valuable advancement. The weaknesses prevent assigning a higher score, as further validation in different settings and a thorough cost-benefit analysis is needed.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Kronecker-LoRA: hybrid Kronecker-LoRA adapters for scalable, sustainable fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01961v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Shen
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning massive pre-trained language models across many tasks demands adapters that are both parameter-efficient and highly expressive. We introduce \textbf{Kron-LoRA}, a two-stage adapter that first factorizes each frozen linear update as a Kronecker product \[ \Delta W = A \otimes B \] and then compresses \[ B \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{B2}\times d_{B1}} \] via an \(r\)-rank LoRA decomposition \(B \approx B_{1}B_{2}\). By leveraging \[ \mathrm{rank}(A \otimes B) \;=\; \mathrm{rank}(A)\,\mathrm{rank}(B), \] Kron-LoRA retains the expressivity of the update while using up to $4\!\times\!$ fewer parameters than a standard rank-8 LoRA adapter. Its compact adapter matrices also quantize to 8- or 4-bit with less accuracy degradation than LoRA, enabling further memory and storage savings for on-device deployment. We benchmark on DistilBERT and Mistral-7B across five tasks (PIQA, HellaSwag, WinoGrande, ARC-Easy, ARC-Challenge) over multiple epochs of adapter-only tuning: on DistilBERT, an 840 K-parameter Kron-LoRA matches LoRA-16's performance, and on Mistral-7B, a 5.7 M-parameter Kron-LoRA rivals LoRA-8 with modest memory savings and only a 3-8\% speed overhead. In sequential fine-tuning from ARC-Challenge to ARC-Easy, Kron-LoRA retains 55.18\% accuracy versus 53.17\% for LoRA-8-despite using only one-quarter of the adapter parameters-underscoring its competitive cross-task transfer performance. By uniting Kronecker structure, low-rank compression, quantization-friendliness, and by providing transparent trade-off analysis, Kron-LoRA offers a scalable, sustainable, and continual-learning-ready solution for multi-task adaptation of large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Kron-LoRA, a parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) method for large language models (LLMs).  Kron-LoRA combines Kronecker product factorization with low-rank LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation).  It factorizes the weight update matrix as a Kronecker product and then applies a LoRA decomposition to one of the factors. This approach aims to reduce the number of trainable parameters while maintaining expressiveness. The authors demonstrate through experiments on DistilBERT and Mistral-7B, across several benchmark tasks, that Kron-LoRA achieves comparable or better performance than standard LoRA with significantly fewer parameters. They also analyze quantization properties, showing Kron-LoRA is more quantization-friendly. Finally, they investigate continual learning scenarios, evaluating how well Kron-LoRA retains knowledge when fine-tuned sequentially on different tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of combining Kronecker factorization with LoRA isn't entirely new, as previous work has explored Kronecker products in adapter modules (e.g., KronA). However, Kron-LoRA's specific two-stage approach (Kronecker factorization followed by LoRA compression) and its demonstrated practical benefits are novel. The quantization analysis is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** Parameter-efficient fine-tuning is a crucial area in LLM research due to the prohibitive costs associated with full fine-tuning. Kron-LoRA presents a practical and effective method for reducing the adapter size, which is vital for deployment in resource-constrained environments (edge devices, federated learning). The consistent experimental results across multiple models and tasks strengthen the significance of the findings. The continual learning analysis highlights both the strengths and weaknesses of the method, providing valuable insights for future research. The discussion about the method's quantization properties is also significant, making the model more appropriate for deployment.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Parameter efficiency:**  Kron-LoRA achieves significant parameter reduction while maintaining performance, which is its primary strength.
    *   **Quantization-friendly:** The analysis and evidence suggesting better quantization properties compared to LoRA are valuable.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The paper provides results on various models, tasks, and evaluation metrics (accuracy, throughput, memory), offering a comprehensive picture of Kron-LoRA's performance.
    *   **Continual learning analysis:**  The exploration of forgetting and retention in sequential fine-tuning is important.
    *   **Clear explanation:** The paper clearly explains the method and provides intuitive justifications.
    *   **Broader Applicability:** Discussion on Broader applicability beyond NLP is well articulated.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Speed overhead:** The paper acknowledges a modest speed overhead (3–8%) due to the additional matrix operations. While acceptable, this needs to be considered in real-time applications.
    *   **Continual learning limitations:** The paper identifies limitations in continual learning scenarios under domain shifts, suggesting further research is needed in regularization or adapter merging strategies. The performance could be improved further.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** Though the method brings several advantages, the accuracy improvements over existing methods aren't overwhelmingly large. The primary focus appears to be on the reduced adapter size while retaining acceptable performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** Kron-LoRA has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and deployable LLMs. Its quantization-friendly nature is particularly attractive for edge devices and federated learning. The continual learning insights could also spur further research in mitigating forgetting in PEFT methods.

* **Rigour:** All experiments seem rigourous and reproducible.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The score of 7.5 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field of parameter-efficient fine-tuning.  It is not a breakthrough, but it introduces a practical method (Kron-LoRA) with clear advantages in parameter efficiency and quantization properties. The significance is in the demonstrated effectiveness of Kron-LoRA, its scalability, and the identification of its limitations (continual learning under domain shift). The weaknesses prevent a higher score, but the paper's strengths warrant a rating significantly above average. While the individual ideas may not be entirely new, their combination and detailed analysis in the context of LLM fine-tuning make this a valuable contribution with reasonable novelty.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Accelerating LLM Reasoning via Early Rejection with Partial Reward Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01969v1)
- **Authors**: Seyyed Saeid Cheshmi, Azal Ahmad Khan, Xinran Wang, Zirui Liu, Ali Anwar
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly relied upon for solving complex reasoning tasks in domains such as mathematics, logic, and multi-step question answering. A growing line of work seeks to improve reasoning quality by scaling inference time compute particularly through Process Reward Models (PRMs), used to reward the reasoning at intermediate steps. While effective, these methods introduce substantial computational overhead, especially when generating large numbers of solutions in parallel. In this paper, we investigate whether PRMs can be used mid-generation to provide early signals that enable the rejection of suboptimal candidates before full generation of step is complete. We introduce the hypothesis that PRMs are also Partial Reward Models, meaning that the scores they assign to partially completed reasoning step are predictive of final output quality. This allows for principled early rejection based on intermediate token-level signals. We support this hypothesis both theoretically, by proving that the risk of discarding optimal beams decreases exponentially with generation length and empirically, by demonstrating a strong correlation between partial and final rewards across multiple reward models. On math reasoning benchmarks, our method achieves up to 1.4$\times$-9$\times$ reduction in inference FLOPs without degrading final performance. These results suggest that early rejection is a powerful mechanism for improving the compute-efficiency of reasoning in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a method to accelerate large language model (LLM) reasoning by using process reward models (PRMs) for early rejection of suboptimal reasoning paths. The core idea is that PRMs, conventionally used at the end of each reasoning step, can also be used mid-generation to provide partial rewards that are predictive of the final output quality. This allows the system to reject unpromising candidates early, reducing computational overhead. The authors provide theoretical guarantees related to the probability of discarding optimal trajectories and demonstrate empirically that their approach can significantly reduce inference FLOPs without degrading performance on math reasoning benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using PRMs for early rejection is a natural extension of existing work on PRM-guided reasoning. The authors correctly identify the computational bottleneck of full-step evaluation and propose a straightforward yet effective solution. While the core concept isn't revolutionary, the rigorous analysis and empirical validation contribute to the novelty. The theoretical guarantee provides a solid foundation, and the experimental results across multiple models and datasets demonstrate practical applicability.  However, other works such as Sun et al., (2024) in their approach of Speculative Rejection using ORMs for early rejection in BoN already propose early rejections. Compared to this work, which is uses ORMs for early rejection, this work introduces a novel approach that leverages partial rewards in PRMs to achieve the goal of early rejections.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in LLM reasoning: computational efficiency. By reducing the number of tokens generated and PRM evaluations, the method has the potential to significantly reduce the cost of inference for complex reasoning tasks. The empirical results showing FLOP reductions of up to 9x are compelling and suggest that early rejection could be a valuable technique for improving the practicality of PRM-guided reasoning. It makes PRMs more practically applicable.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical validation across multiple LLMs, PRMs, and datasets.
    *   Theoretical guarantee provides a formal justification for the approach.
    *   Addresses a practically important problem in LLM reasoning.
    *   The write-up is quite thorough and accessible.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The assumption of monotonic relationship between the partial and final scores might not hold in all scenarios, limiting the applicability of the method.
    *   The theoretical guarantee relies on specific assumptions about the noise distribution, which might not always be valid in practice.
    *   The experiments are primarily focused on math reasoning, and it is unclear how well the method would generalize to other types of reasoning tasks.  The impact of error types introduced by the approach isn't deeply studied.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on PRM-guided reasoning and other techniques for improving the efficiency of LLMs. The idea of using intermediate signals for early rejection could be extended to other types of models and tasks. It also highlights the importance of considering both accuracy and computational cost when developing reasoning algorithms. The strong experimental findings could also inspire practitioners to adopt early rejection as a practical optimization technique.

*   **Rigor and Justification:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation of their proposed method. They compare to the baseline PRM guided beam search, and demonstrate significant efficiency gains. They thoroughly account for many factors such as datasets, beam sizes and LLM-PRM configrations.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a technically sound and empirically validated method for accelerating LLM reasoning. While the core idea is not a complete paradigm shift, the authors provide sufficient theoretical and experimental evidence to demonstrate its practical value and potential influence. The thorough analysis and comprehensive experimental results justify a score of 7, indicating a strong and valuable contribution to the field, but not an earth-shattering one. A score of 7 reflects that there is a sound contribution with significant novelty, but the work is incremental in nature. The method is clearly useful and improves significantly on the existing state-of-the-art, however it has some significant limitations to consider.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Diffusion models for inverse problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01975v1)
- **Authors**: Hyungjin Chung, Jeongsol Kim, Jong Chul Ye
- **Abstract**: Using diffusion priors to solve inverse problems in imaging have significantly matured over the years. In this chapter, we review the various different approaches that were proposed over the years. We categorize the approaches into the more classic explicit approximation approaches and others, which include variational inference, sequential monte carlo, and decoupled data consistency. We cover the extension to more challenging situations, including blind cases, high-dimensional data, and problems under data scarcity and distribution mismatch. More recent approaches that aim to leverage multimodal information through texts are covered. Through this chapter, we aim to (i) distill the common mathematical threads that connect these algorithms, (ii) systematically contrast their assumptions and performance trade-offs across representative inverse problems, and (iii) spotlight the open theoretical and practical challenges by clarifying the landscape of diffusion model based inverse problem solvers.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper "Diffusion Models for Inverse Problems".

**Summary:**

This chapter provides a comprehensive review of diffusion models and their application to solving inverse problems in imaging and related fields. It categorizes various approaches, including explicit approximation methods (e.g., Diffusion Posterior Sampling - DPS) and more advanced techniques like variational inference, sequential Monte Carlo, and decoupled data consistency. It discusses extensions to handle challenging scenarios like blind inverse problems, high-dimensional data, data scarcity, and distribution mismatch. Furthermore, the chapter delves into leveraging multimodal information, particularly through text conditioning. The authors aim to unify the mathematical underpinnings of these algorithms, contrast their performance trade-offs, and highlight open theoretical and practical challenges in the field. The chapter covers the fundamentals of diffusion models from both score and variational perspectives, then it discusses how to sample from the reverse trajectory using score functions, explicit approximation and so on.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its organization and synthesis of the existing literature. While the individual algorithms and techniques discussed are not new, the chapter offers a structured overview and classification that is helpful for researchers in the field. The way the approaches are categorized, specifically highlighting the distinction between explicit approximations and other principled methods, is a valuable contribution. Furthermore, the inclusion of very recent (mid-2025) papers, even those still on arXiv, is a strength.

**Significance:** The significance of this review stems from the increasing prominence of diffusion models in solving inverse problems. The field has grown rapidly, and a comprehensive overview is beneficial for both newcomers and experienced researchers. By clarifying the connections and trade-offs between different methods, the chapter can guide future research directions and facilitate the development of more effective algorithms.  The identification of open challenges (e.g., theoretical understanding, computational efficiency, handling complex forward models) is also valuable.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The chapter covers a wide range of approaches, providing a good overview of the field.
*   **Clear Categorization:** The categorization of methods into explicit approximations and other principled approaches is insightful.
*   **Up-to-Date:** The inclusion of recent publications (even preprints) makes the review timely and relevant.
*   **Emphasis on Trade-offs:** The focus on contrasting assumptions and performance trade-offs is helpful for practical applications.
*   **Identification of Open Challenges:** Highlighting open problems can stimulate further research.
*   **Organization and Structure:** The logical organization, beginning with background and proceeding through various categories of methods, enhances readability and accessibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Original Research:** The chapter is primarily a review and does not present any significant new theoretical or experimental results.
*   **Depth of Analysis:** While the coverage is broad, the depth of analysis of individual methods could be greater. A more critical comparison of the performance of different methods on specific benchmark problems would be beneficial.
*   **Potential for Bias:** As with any review, there is a potential for bias in the selection and interpretation of the literature.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a solid contribution by providing a well-organized, comprehensive, and up-to-date review of a rapidly evolving field. While it lacks original research and the depth of analysis in some areas could be improved, its value as a resource for researchers in diffusion models and inverse problems is significant. The categorization of methods, the emphasis on trade-offs, and the identification of open challenges make it a valuable addition to the literature. Its comprehensive nature and relatively up-to-date information makes the paper an important resource, but its lack of original research limits its novelty within the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### TIBSTC-CoT: A Multi-Domain Instruction Dataset for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01977v1)
- **Authors**: Fan Gao, Cheng Huang, Nyima Tashi, Yutong Liu, Xiangxiang Wang, Thupten Tsering, Ban Ma-bao, Renzeg Duojie, Gadeng Luosang, Rinchen Dongrub, Dorje Tashi, Xiao Feng, Hao Wang, Yongbin Yu
- **Abstract**: To address the severe data scarcity in Tibetan, a low-resource language spoken by over six million people, we introduce TIBSTC-CoT, the large-scale, multi-domain Tibetan dataset automatically constructed via chain-of-thought prompting with large language models (LLMs). TIBSTC-CoT establishes a scalable and reproducible framework for dataset creation in low-resource settings, covering diverse domains and reasoning patterns essential for language understanding and generation. Building on this dataset, we develop the Sunshine-thinking LLM family, a series of Tibetan-centric LLMs equipped with chain-of-thought capabilities. Trained entirely on TIBSTC-CoT, Sunshine-thinking has demonstrated strong reasoning and generation performance, comparable to state-of-the-art (SOTA) multilingual LLMs. Our work marks a significant step toward inclusive AI by enabling high-quality Tibetan language processing through both resource creation and model innovation. All data are available: https://github.com/Vicentvankor/sun-shine.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TIBSTC-CoT, a large-scale, multi-domain Tibetan instruction dataset designed to address the severe data scarcity in Tibetan NLP. The dataset is automatically constructed using chain-of-thought prompting with large language models (LLMs). The authors establish a scalable and reproducible framework for creating datasets in low-resource environments and use this to train the Sunshine-thinking LLM family, a series of Tibetan-centric LLMs enhanced with chain-of-thought capabilities.  Experiments show that the Sunshine-thinking models achieve strong reasoning and generation performance, comparable to state-of-the-art multilingual LLMs, even when compared to models specifically designed for high-resource languages. The work highlights the effectiveness of a data-driven approach to improving NLP in underrepresented languages by leveraging LLMs and instruction tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in creating the first large-scale, multi-domain Tibetan instruction dataset with chain-of-thought reasoning. While the methodology of using LLMs to generate datasets is not entirely new, its application to Tibetan, a severely under-resourced language, is a significant and practical contribution. The creation of the Sunshine-Thinking family, specifically tailored for Tibetan using the generated dataset, adds to the novelty. However, the use of SFT (Supervised Fine Tuning) is a well known method with clear limitations.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant for several reasons:
    *   **Addressing Data Scarcity:**  It directly tackles the data scarcity problem in Tibetan NLP, a major bottleneck hindering progress.
    *   **Promoting Language Inclusivity:** It contributes to inclusive AI by enabling high-quality Tibetan language processing, helping to bridge the digital divide in multilingual NLP.
    *   **Scalable Framework:** The automatic dataset generation pipeline provides a framework applicable to other low-resource languages, offering a replicable blueprint for resource creation.
    *   **Practical Impact:** Demonstrating competitive performance with SOTA multilingual LLMs highlights the potential of this approach to improve real-world applications for Tibetan speakers.
    *   **CoT in Tibetan:** The introduction of Chain-of-Thought methods to Tibetan NLP is also a strong contribution, as it encourages both better explainability of LLM decision-making, as well as improved understanding of the reasoning process.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The release of TIBSTC-CoT is a tangible and valuable resource for the Tibetan NLP community.
    *   **Practical Methodology:** The dataset generation pipeline is well-defined and automatable.
    *   **Strong Results:** The competitive performance of the Sunshine-thinking models, especially compared to LLMs from high-resource environments, validates the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methods and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Existing LLMs:** The dataset generation relies on the performance of existing multilingual LLMs, which may introduce biases.  Also, if the pre-existing LLMs improve, the pipeline will be able to create increasingly high-quality datasets.
    *   **Evaluation Limitations:** While the TLUE benchmark and chrF++ metrics provide valuable insights, additional evaluations on downstream tasks (e.g., translation, summarization) could further demonstrate the practical utility of the models.
    *   **Limited Innovation in LLM Training:** The Sunshine-Thinking models are built using established fine-tuning techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a strong potential influence on the field by:
    *   **Inspiring Similar Work:** It can encourage similar efforts for other under-resourced languages.
    *   **Facilitating Tibetan NLP Research:** It provides a critical resource for researchers working on Tibetan language processing.
    *   **Advancing Multilingual NLP:** It demonstrates the effectiveness of targeted data-driven approaches in improving performance for underrepresented languages.

Overall, the paper presents a valuable and significant contribution to the field of multilingual NLP, especially for low-resource languages. The creation of the TIBSTC-CoT dataset and the Sunshine-thinking models addresses a critical need and provides a practical pathway to improving Tibetan language processing. While the reliance on existing LLMs introduces potential biases, the results demonstrate the efficacy of the approach and its potential for wider application.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
The paper's significance and practical impact is quite high due to it addressing a major lack of data for under-resourced languages. However, the techniques are well known and the paper is likely to have greater impact within its local community rather than the larger NLP community. Given these caveats, a score of 7 reflects this evaluation well, and also encourages the team to look into more novel applications for their novel dataset.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prefill-Decode Aggregation or Disaggregation? Unifying Both for Goodput-Optimized LLM Serving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01989v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Wang, Pengfei Zuo, Zhangyu Chen, Yunkai Liang, Zhou Yu, Ming-Chang Yang
- **Abstract**: An ongoing debate considers whether prefill-decode (PD) aggregation or disaggregation is superior for serving large language models (LLMs). This has driven optimizations for both approaches, each showing distinct advantages. This paper compares PD aggregation and disaggregation, showing that each excels under different service-level objectives (SLOs): aggregation is optimal for tight time-to-first-token (TTFT) and relaxed time-per-output-token (TPOT), while disaggregation excels for strict TPOT and relaxed TTFT. However, under balanced TTFT and TPOT SLOs, neither approach delivers optimal goodput. This paper proposes TaiChi, an LLM serving system that unifies PD disaggregation and aggregation for optimal goodput under any combination of TTFT and TPOT SLOs. TaiChi uses a unified disaggregation-aggregation architecture with differentiated-capability GPU instances: prefill-heavy (fast prefill, high-interference decode) and decode-heavy (low-interference decode, slow prefill). Three configurable sliders control the ratio between these instances and their chunk sizes. TaiChi adapts to various SLO regimes by adjusting sliders. When TTFT constraints are tight, TaiChi resembles a PD aggregation configuration; when TPOT dominates, it adapts toward PD disaggregation. Crucially, under balanced SLOs, TaiChi enables a hybrid mode for superior goodput. The key innovation behind this hybrid mode is latency shifting: selectively reallocating GPU resources from requests that meet SLOs to those at risk of violation, maximizing the number of SLO-satisfied requests. This fine-grained latency shifting is orchestrated by two scheduling mechanisms: flowing decode scheduling to control TPOTs and length-aware prefill scheduling to manage TTFTs, which jointly optimize request assignment. Our experiments show TaiChi improves goodput by up to 77% over state-of-the-art systems under balanced TTFT and TPOT SLOs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the ongoing debate regarding prefill-decode (PD) aggregation versus disaggregation for serving Large Language Models (LLMs). It argues that neither approach is universally superior, with aggregation excelling under tight time-to-first-token (TTFT) constraints and relaxed time-per-output-token (TPOT) requirements, while disaggregation performs better with strict TPOT and relaxed TTFT constraints. The core contribution is TaiChi, a novel LLM serving system that unifies PD aggregation and disaggregation, offering optimal goodput under various TTFT and TPOT combinations. TaiChi employs a unified architecture consisting of prefill-heavy (fast prefill, high-interference decode) and decode-heavy (slow prefill, low-interference decode) GPU instances. It provides configurable sliders to control the ratio between these instance types and their chunk sizes, enabling adaptation to different SLO regimes.  The system leverages "latency shifting," selectively reallocating GPU resources to requests at risk of violating SLOs by implementing flowing decode scheduling and length-aware prefill scheduling. Experimental results show significant goodput improvements compared to state-of-the-art systems under balanced TTFT and TPOT SLOs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the unified architecture that combines aspects of both PD aggregation and disaggregation, along with the "latency shifting" mechanism. While the individual concepts of aggregation and disaggregation are well-established, the authors' approach of integrating them dynamically and strategically reallocating resources based on SLO urgency is a significant contribution. The configurable sliders offer a degree of flexibility not found in rigid aggregation or disaggregation strategies. The Flowing Decode Scheduling and Length-Aware Prefill Scheduling algorithms provide a practical mechanism for achieving this latency shifting.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and important problem in LLM serving: optimizing goodput while satisfying diverse SLO requirements. The identification of the trade-offs between TTFT and TPOT and the limitations of existing approaches is insightful. The proposed solution, TaiChi, demonstrates tangible performance improvements, making it a valuable contribution to the field. The experimental evaluation is reasonably comprehensive, comparing against established baselines and demonstrating effectiveness across different workloads and SLO constraints.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Insightful analysis of the trade-offs between PD aggregation and disaggregation.
    *   Novel architecture and scheduling mechanisms.
    *   Strong experimental results demonstrating significant performance gains.
    *   Well-written and well-structured paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The system relies on offline search for optimal configuration, and while the paper mentions an on-demand search-and-reconfigure strategy, the details of this dynamic adaptation mechanism are not fully elaborated in this paper. More insight into the automation process for selecting optimal configurations given changes in workload characteristics would be helpful.
    *   The experimental evaluation, while comprehensive, is limited to a specific hardware setup (8 A100 GPUs) and two specific LLM models. Additional testing on different hardware configurations and model architectures would strengthen the findings.
    *   The complexity of the system. While the paper provides a clear explanation of the overall architecture, more practical details on the implementation and deployment challenges would be helpful for other researchers and practitioners.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM serving. The proposed TaiChi system offers a promising solution to the problem of optimizing goodput under diverse SLO constraints by unifying aggregation and disaggregation strategies and introducing a latency-shifting mechanism. While some aspects, like the dynamic configuration selection and broader hardware testing, could be further elaborated, the core ideas and the demonstrated performance improvements warrant a high score.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 8/10

### Toward Efficient Spiking Transformers: Synapse Pruning Meets Synergistic Learning-Based Compensation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01992v1)
- **Authors**: Hongze Sun, Wuque Cai, Duo Chen, Shifeng Mao, Jiayi He, Zhenxing Wang, Dezhong Yao, Daqing Guo
- **Abstract**: As a foundational architecture of artificial intelligence models, Transformer has been recently adapted to spiking neural networks with promising performance across various tasks. However, existing spiking Transformer (ST)-based models require a substantial number of parameters and incur high computational costs, thus limiting their deployment in resource-constrained environments. To address these challenges, we propose combining synapse pruning with a synergistic learning-based compensation strategy to derive lightweight ST-based models. Specifically, two types of tailored pruning strategies are introduced to reduce redundancy in the weight matrices of ST blocks: an unstructured $\mathrm{L_{1}P}$ method to induce sparse representations, and a structured DSP method to induce low-rank representations. In addition, we propose an enhanced spiking neuron model, termed the synergistic leaky integrate-and-fire (sLIF) neuron, to effectively compensate for model pruning through synergistic learning between synaptic and intrinsic plasticity mechanisms. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed methods significantly reduce model size and computational overhead while maintaining competitive performance. These results validate the effectiveness of the proposed pruning and compensation strategies in constructing efficient and high-performing ST-based models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Toward Efficient Spiking Transformers: Synapse Pruning Meets Synergistic Learning-Based Compensation":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the computational and parameter inefficiency of spiking transformer (ST) models. It proposes a method to create lightweight ST models by combining synapse pruning techniques with a synergistic learning-based compensation strategy. The approach uses two customized pruning strategies: an unstructured L1-norm based parameter sparsification (L1P) and a structured dimension significance-based pruning (DSP). To compensate for performance degradation caused by pruning, the authors introduce an enhanced spiking neuron model called the synergistic leaky integrate-and-fire (sLIF) neuron. The sLIF neuron incorporates both synaptic and intrinsic plasticity mechanisms, learned synergistically to recover lost performance. The paper presents experimental results on various datasets demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed method in reducing model size and computational overhead while maintaining competitive performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of combining synapse pruning with synergistic learning for compensation is novel in the context of spiking transformers. The introduction of the sLIF neuron model, explicitly learning intrinsic plasticity parameters alongside synaptic weights to compensate for pruning, is a noteworthy contribution. The tailored pruning strategies (L1P and DSP) for ST blocks also add to the novelty. Prior works have explored either pruning or enhanced neuron models, but the integrated approach is a distinguishing feature.

* **Significance:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in the deployment of SNNs, particularly spiking transformers, which are inherently computationally expensive. Reducing the size and computational cost of ST models while preserving performance is crucial for their adoption in resource-constrained environments. If the results presented are robust and generalize well to different tasks and datasets, the proposed method has the potential to significantly improve the practicality of ST models. The paper provides a potentially more general approach for ST compression, rather than architecture-specific techniques.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper provides a clear and well-structured description of the proposed method.
    * The experimental results demonstrate substantial reductions in model size and computational overhead.
    * The integration of synergistic learning into the spiking neuron model is a promising technique.
    * The method is model-agnostic (claimed) and can be integrated into existing ST-based models.
    * The paper has well-presented quantitative results on various datasets (ImageNet-100, CIFAR10, CIFAR10-DVS).

* **Weaknesses:**
    * While claimed model-agnostic, the specifics of integrating this with existing ST frameworks still seem vague and needs to be more explicit.
    * The experimental validation, while promising, needs more details. Especially for the large-scale dataset, the absolute accuracy compared to *dense* models needs to be much more competitive for practicality.
    * The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the synergistic learning process. What specific dynamics emerge during the simultaneous learning of synaptic and intrinsic parameters, and how do these dynamics contribute to performance recovery after pruning? What is the sensitivity of the performance to initialization/learning rates of intrinsic parameters?
    * More extensive ablation studies could further illuminate the contribution of individual components (e.g., sLIF alone without pruning).
    * How robust is the method to different pre-training conditions, or pre-training methods? Does it need specialized pre-training techniques?

* **Potential Influence:** If validated through further research, the proposed method could become a standard approach for creating efficient spiking transformer models. It could also inspire further research into synergistic learning techniques for SNNs. Widespread adoption of this kind of technique would accelerate the research and deployment of SNNs, especially in the edge.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to a crucial problem in the field of SNNs. The idea of combining pruning with synergistic learning-based compensation offers a promising pathway towards efficient and practical ST models. However, the paper has some limitations, specifically the vague description of model integration, requiring more comprehensive experimental validation, and the need for more in-depth analysis of the synergistic learning process. While promising results are demonstrated, more details and experiments are needed to fully prove its robustness and generality. Given these considerations, I assign a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompting Large Language Models to Detect Dementia Family Caregivers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01999v1)
- **Authors**: Md Badsha Biswas, Özlem Uzuner
- **Abstract**: Social media, such as Twitter, provides opportunities for caregivers of dementia patients to share their experiences and seek support for a variety of reasons. Availability of this information online also paves the way for the development of internet-based interventions in their support. However, for this purpose, tweets written by caregivers of dementia patients must first be identified. This paper demonstrates our system for the SMM4H 2025 shared task 3, which focuses on detecting tweets posted by individuals who have a family member with dementia. The task is outlined as a binary classification problem, differentiating between tweets that mention dementia in the context of a family member and those that do not. Our solution to this problem explores large language models (LLMs) with various prompting methods. Our results show that a simple zero-shot prompt on a fine-tuned model yielded the best results. Our final system achieved a macro F1-score of 0.95 on the validation set and the test set. Our full code is available on GitHub.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary**

The paper describes a system developed for the SMM4H 2025 shared task 3, which focuses on detecting tweets written by individuals who are family caregivers of dementia patients. The system uses instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, fine-tuned with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). The authors experimented with different prompting strategies (zero-shot, few-shot, chain-of-thought, and cascaded) and found that a simple zero-shot prompt with an oversampled, fine-tuned model yielded the best performance, achieving an F1-score of 0.95 on both the validation and test sets. The paper also touches upon data preparation techniques (oversampling to address class imbalance) and error analysis.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Strengths:*

*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper addresses a relevant and important problem: identifying dementia family caregivers on social media. This is crucial for developing targeted online interventions.
*   **Reasonable Methodology:** The use of LLMs fine-tuned with LoRA is a good choice, given the resource constraints and the effectiveness of parameter-efficient fine-tuning. The consideration of different prompting strategies is also a positive aspect.
*   **Good Performance:**  The reported F1-score of 0.95 is competitive, suggesting a reasonably well-performing system.
*   **Error Analysis:** The inclusion of an error analysis section is a strength, as it provides insights into the system's limitations and potential avenues for improvement.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Novelty:** The methodology itself isn't particularly novel. Applying LLMs and LoRA to text classification is a well-established approach. The different prompting strategies, while evaluated, are also standard techniques. While the error analysis identified that larger coreference-aware models could benefit from improving implicit relationship detections, it appears the study was limited in the scope of exploring the limitations of the current models.
*   **Lack of In-Depth Comparison:** While they mention BERTweet as a benchmark, a more detailed comparison (statistical significance testing, analysis of cases where each model succeeds/fails) would strengthen the paper.  Simply stating the performance relative to the benchmark is insufficient.
*   **Limited Exploration of Model Size:** The paper mentions the potential benefit of larger models (e.g., 70B) but doesn't explore this avenue. The justification is understandable (resource constraints), but it limits the scope of the findings. There were limitations in the scope of exploring the limitations of the current models.
*   **Dataset is SMM4H2025 provided:** The dataset provided is not novel, making the contributions in regards to novel data limited.

*Significance:*

The paper contributes to the literature by:

*   Providing a practical application of LLMs to a specific healthcare-related task.
*   Comparing different prompting strategies in the context of this task.
*   Providing insights into the challenges of classifying social media data related to dementia caregiving.

However, the *incremental* contribution to the field of NLP is limited due to the reliance on existing techniques and a lack of in-depth analysis. While the application is useful, the methodological novelty is low. The paper confirms that LLMs can be used to classify caregiver tweets with reasonable accuracy, but doesn't offer a substantial breakthrough in terms of methodology or understanding.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper could be useful for:

*   Researchers working on similar healthcare-related text classification tasks.
*   Practitioners interested in applying LLMs to social media data analysis.
*   Participants in future SMM4H shared tasks.

However, its limited novelty reduces its potential to have a significant or lasting impact on the field of NLP.

**Justification for the Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **5**. Here's why:

*   The paper addresses a relevant problem and demonstrates a practical application of LLMs. However, the methodology is largely based on existing techniques, with limited novel contributions. While the achieved performance is good, it does not present any significant breakthroughs. The lack of coreference resolution exploration also limits the contribution to the field. A more rigorous comparison of different prompting strategies and models, along with a deeper analysis of the error cases, would have strengthened the paper and justified a higher score. The dataset is also not unique, resulting in limited contributions.

Score: 5

- **Score**: 5/10

### Convolutions are Competitive with Transformers for Encrypted Traffic Classification with Pre-training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02001v1)
- **Authors**: Chungang Lin, Weiyao Zhang, Tianyu Zuo, Chao Zha, Yilong Jiang, Ruiqi Meng, Haitong Luo, Xuying Meng, Yujun Zhang
- **Abstract**: Encrypted traffic classification is vital for modern network management and security. To reduce reliance on handcrafted features and labeled data, recent methods focus on learning generic representations through pre-training on large-scale unlabeled data. However, current pre-trained models face two limitations originating from the adopted Transformer architecture: (1) Limited model efficiency due to the self-attention mechanism with quadratic complexity; (2) Unstable traffic scalability to longer byte sequences, as the explicit positional encodings fail to generalize to input lengths not seen during pre-training. In this paper, we investigate whether convolutions, with linear complexity and implicit positional encoding, are competitive with Transformers in encrypted traffic classification with pre-training. We first conduct a systematic comparison, and observe that convolutions achieve higher efficiency and scalability, with lower classification performance. To address this trade-off, we propose NetConv, a novel pre-trained convolution model for encrypted traffic classification. NetConv employs stacked traffic convolution layers, which enhance the ability to capture localized byte-sequence patterns through window-wise byte scoring and sequence-wise byte gating. We design a continuous byte masking pre-training task to help NetConv learn protocol-specific patterns. Experimental results on four tasks demonstrate that NetConv improves average classification performance by 6.88% and model throughput by 7.41X over existing pre-trained models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Convolutions are Competitive with Transformers for Encrypted Traffic Classification with Pre-training" investigates the potential of convolutional neural networks (CNNs) as a competitive alternative to Transformers for encrypted traffic classification, particularly when using pre-training techniques. The authors identify two key limitations of Transformers in this context: limited model efficiency due to quadratic complexity and unstable traffic scalability with longer byte sequences due to fixed positional encoding. They propose NetConv, a novel pre-trained convolutional model that employs stacked traffic convolution layers, window-wise byte scoring (WBS), sequence-wise byte gating (SBG), and a continuous byte masking (CBM) pre-training task to enhance the capture of localized byte sequence patterns. The authors present experimental results on four tasks demonstrating that NetConv improves classification performance and model throughput compared to existing pre-trained models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of using CNNs for traffic classification isn't entirely new, the paper's novelty lies in the specific architecture (NetConv) and the comprehensive exploration of CNNs with pre-training in this specific domain, coupled with a direct comparison against state-of-the-art Transformer-based methods. The design of NetConv, incorporating WBS, SBG, and CBM, represents a non-trivial contribution aimed at addressing the limitations of standard CNNs in capturing the complex sequential patterns present in encrypted traffic. The systematic comparison between CNNs and Transformers, highlighting their respective strengths and weaknesses related to efficiency, scalability, and accuracy, is valuable.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical problem of increasing importance: efficient and accurate encrypted traffic classification. The findings suggest that CNNs, often overlooked in favor of Transformers due to their perceived limitations in capturing long-range dependencies, can be effectively leveraged for this task with proper design and pre-training. The significant improvement in model throughput (claimed 7.41x) over Transformer-based methods could have a real impact on the feasibility of deploying such models in resource-constrained environments. The few-shot learning experiments are also significant, suggesting that NetConv can achieve good performance with considerably fewer labeled samples, which is crucial in real-world scenarios where labeled data is scarce. The thorough ablation study convincingly demonstrates the importance of the individual components of the proposed architecture. The focus on the issues that are important such as efficiency and throughput make the research very practically-oriented.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing Transformer-based approaches.
    *   **Systematic Comparison:**  The comparison between CNNs and Transformers is well-structured and provides valuable insights.
    *   **Novel Architecture:** The proposed NetConv architecture is well-motivated and demonstrably effective.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, using multiple datasets, metrics, and ablation studies.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on model efficiency and few-shot learning directly addresses real-world deployment challenges.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Ablation:** A more detailed investigation of the impact of hyperparameter choices within the different modules would be valuable.
    *   **Dataset Diversity:** While four datasets were used, exploring performance across an even broader range of traffic types and network conditions would strengthen the generalizability claims.
    *   **Comparison Choice:** While ET-BERT, NetGPT, TrafficFormer are reasonable choices, including more CNN based baselines could be helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Re-evaluating the role of CNNs in traffic classification, especially with pre-training.
    *   Providing a practical alternative to resource-intensive Transformers.
    *   Guiding future research towards more efficient and scalable traffic classification models.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated CNN-based model (NetConv) for encrypted traffic classification that addresses key limitations of Transformer-based approaches. While there are some minor weaknesses, the strengths of the paper outweigh them, and it represents a significant contribution to the field. The paper offers a practical and efficient approach, backed by solid experimental evidence, and has the potential to influence future research and deployment strategies.

**Score: 8**

The paper makes a valuable, novel contribution to the area. It is well-motivated, methodologically sound, and provides strong experimental evidence that it addresses limitations of current approaches. While there are some minor weaknesses, particularly regarding the breadth of datasets, the paper's strength in architectural design, clear demonstration of improved efficiency, and thorough evaluation warrant a score of 8.

- **Classification**: cs.NI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Generative Large-Scale Pre-trained Models for Automated Ad Bidding Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02002v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Lei, Jiayang Zhao, Yilei Zhao, Zhaoqi Zhang, Linyou Cai, Qianlong Xie, Xingxing Wang
- **Abstract**: Modern auto-bidding systems are required to balance overall performance with diverse advertiser goals and real-world constraints, reflecting the dynamic and evolving needs of the industry. Recent advances in conditional generative models, such as transformers and diffusers, have enabled direct trajectory generation tailored to advertiser preferences, offering a promising alternative to traditional Markov Decision Process-based methods. However, these generative methods face significant challenges, such as the distribution shift between offline and online environments, limited exploration of the action space, and the necessity to meet constraints like marginal Cost-per-Mille (CPM) and Return on Investment (ROI). To tackle these challenges, we propose GRAD (Generative Reward-driven Ad-bidding with Mixture-of-Experts), a scalable foundation model for auto-bidding that combines an Action-Mixture-of-Experts module for diverse bidding action exploration with the Value Estimator of Causal Transformer for constraint-aware optimization. Extensive offline and online experiments demonstrate that GRAD significantly enhances platform revenue, highlighting its effectiveness in addressing the evolving and diverse requirements of modern advertisers. Furthermore, GRAD has been implemented in multiple marketing scenarios at Meituan, one of the world's largest online food delivery platforms, leading to a 2.18% increase in Gross Merchandise Value (GMV) and 10.68% increase in ROI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces GRAD (Generative Reward-driven Ad-bidding with Mixture-of-Experts), a novel and scalable foundation model for automated ad bidding. GRAD addresses the challenges of offline-online distribution mismatch, limited action space exploration, and constraint satisfaction (e.g., CPM, ROI) in generative auto-bidding systems. The architecture combines an Action-Mixture-of-Experts module for diverse bidding action exploration and a Causal Transformer Value Estimator for constraint-aware optimization.  Experiments on the AuctionNet dataset and online A/B testing on Meituan (a large online food delivery platform) demonstrate significant improvements in platform revenue, Gross Merchandise Value (GMV), and Return on Investment (ROI). The paper emphasizes the practical deployability and effectiveness of GRAD in real-world advertising environments.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of several existing techniques in a novel way for the specific application of auto-bidding.  The use of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) for exploration, the causal transformer for value estimation, and the overall reward-driven generative approach are all contributions. However, each component individually has precedents in other machine learning applications. The particular combination of these, and their adaptation to handle the specific constraints of auto-bidding is what constitutes the novelty. In this specific context of ad-bidding, the integration of those components constitutes an incremental and potentially significant improvement.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is supported by the promising results on both the AuctionNet dataset and, more importantly, the online A/B testing on Meituan's platform.  A 2.18% increase in GMV and a 10.68% increase in ROI are substantial improvements in a large-scale, real-world advertising system. This demonstrates the practical value and potential for impact of the proposed method. The emphasis on constraint satisfaction, a critical requirement for real-world deployment, further enhances the significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper tackles a relevant and important problem in online advertising.
    *   **Real-World Validation:**  The deployment and evaluation on Meituan's platform provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of GRAD.
    *   **Technical Soundness:**  The paper presents a clear and well-defined architecture.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The experiments include offline evaluations, ablation studies, and online A/B testing.
    *   **Clear writing and organization:** The paper is well-written and relatively easy to follow given the technical nature of the subject.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** As mentioned earlier, the individual components of GRAD are not entirely novel. The main contribution is the integration and adaptation of existing techniques.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** While Meituan is a large platform, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion on the generalizability of GRAD to other advertising environments with different characteristics. Are there particular types of businesses or ad formats that GRAD might be especially suited for, or where it would underperform?
    *   **Dataset Reliance on AuctionNet and Limited Public Datasets:** Although the use of AuctionNet is laudable for its public accessibility, there's a reliance on a single dataset. More rigorous testing across a variety of datasets or a more detailed study of AuctionNet's limitations would be useful. The difficulty in accessing diverse ad-bidding datasets is acknowledged, but it still represents a potential weakness.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field of auto-bidding by demonstrating the effectiveness of generative models and the importance of constraint-aware optimization. It may inspire further research on combining different machine learning techniques for solving real-world advertising problems. The scalability considerations are also relevant and likely to drive adoption.

*   **Broader Impact:** This is a double-edged sword.  While the potential to improve advertising efficiency is good, there are ethical concerns related to the manipulation of consumer behavior and the potential for biased or unfair targeting. These aspects are not discussed.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the points above, I assign the paper a score of 7. The work demonstrates a practically significant improvement in auto-bidding by combining elements of existing techinques. While it doesn't introduce entirely novel machine learning methods, the successful integration and adaptation for real-world ad optimization, coupled with strong empirical results on a large-scale platform, warrants this score. The main factors that limit it from a higher rating are the somewhat incremental nature of the novelty, limitations to generalizability, and some absence of discussion for the broader ethical concerns.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Devil is in the Detail: Towards Injecting Fine Details of Image Prompt in Image Generation via Conflict-free Guidance and Stratified Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02004v1)
- **Authors**: Kyungmin Jo, Jooyeol Yun, Jaegul Choo
- **Abstract**: While large-scale text-to-image diffusion models enable the generation of high-quality, diverse images from text prompts, these prompts struggle to capture intricate details, such as textures, preventing the user intent from being reflected. This limitation has led to efforts to generate images conditioned on user-provided images, referred to as image prompts. Recent work modifies the self-attention mechanism to impose image conditions in generated images by replacing or concatenating the keys and values from the image prompt. This enables the self-attention layer to work like a cross-attention layer, generally used to incorporate text prompts. In this paper, we identify two common issues in existing methods of modifying self-attention to generate images that reflect the details of image prompts. First, existing approaches neglect the importance of image prompts in classifier-free guidance. Specifically, current methods use image prompts as both desired and undesired conditions in classifier-free guidance, causing conflicting signals. To resolve this, we propose conflict-free guidance by using image prompts only as desired conditions, ensuring that the generated image faithfully reflects the image prompt. In addition, we observe that the two most common self-attention modifications involve a trade-off between the realism of the generated image and alignment with the image prompt. Specifically, selecting more keys and values from the image prompt improves alignment, while selecting more from the generated image enhances realism. To balance both, we propose an new self-attention modification method, Stratified Attention to jointly use keys and values from both images rather than selecting between them. Through extensive experiments across three image generation tasks, we show that the proposed method outperforms existing image-prompting models in faithfully reflecting the image prompt.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of injecting fine details from image prompts into image generation using diffusion models. It identifies two main issues with existing self-attention modification techniques: conflicting guidance (using the image prompt for both positive and negative guidance in classifier-free guidance) and a realism-alignment trade-off. To address these, the paper proposes "conflict-free guidance" (using the image prompt only for positive guidance) and "stratified attention" (separately computing attention scores for image prompts and generated features before combining them).  Experiments across various tasks (image variation, cross-prompt generation, and structure-guided generation) demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing image-prompting models in reflecting image prompt details.  The paper provides qualitative and quantitative results, analyzes the impact of different components, and shows generalization across different diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. The idea of using image prompts as conditions is explored by previous works (IP-Adapter, RIVAL, etc), but this paper clearly identifies and tackles two specific problems in existing methods: conflicting guidance and attention bias, introducing novel techniques (conflict-free guidance and stratified attention) to overcome these issues. While self-attention modifications are not entirely new, the specific combination and application to image prompting are novel.
* **Significance:** The paper has notable significance.  Faithfully reflecting user intent in image generation is a crucial research direction. The identified problems and proposed solutions directly improve the ability of diffusion models to incorporate fine-grained details from image prompts. This is beneficial to various applications like image editing, style transfer, etc. The detailed analysis and experiments contribute a deeper understanding of the limitations of current approaches and ways to overcome them. The identified conflict-free guidance seems to be a practical and important guidance adjustment that might be easily adaptable and widely applicable.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Identification:**  The paper clearly articulates two specific issues plaguing current methods.
    * **Technical Approach:**  The proposed solutions (conflict-free guidance, stratified attention) are well-motivated and technically sound.
    * **Experimental Validation:**  The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments (qualitative and quantitative) that supports the effectiveness of the proposed method across different datasets and tasks. It thoroughly analyzes the impact of each component.
    * **Generalization Studies**: Assessing generalization across different diffusion models is a strong point, highlighting the robustness of the approach.
    * **Code Availability**: The code is made publicly available for easy evaluation and future work.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The improvements, while notable, are incremental. The paper builds upon existing self-attention modification techniques, refining them rather than presenting a completely revolutionary approach.
    * While the experiments are thorough, more quantitative comparison in other tasks like image editing or style transfer where image prompts may be used is recommended.
    * The implementation details might require the user to have a certain degree of familiarity with the underlying diffusion models.
    * Even though the proposed techniques improves the performance, there is no theoretical analysis of why and how Stratified Attention contributes to better performance in self-attention models.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by highlighting the importance of conflict-free guidance and introducing a practical attention modification technique. Future work could build upon this foundation, exploring other ways to improve the fidelity and controllability of image generation from image prompts. The proposed conflict-free guidance could be more widely adopted in future image prompt related works.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.  The paper makes a solid contribution by identifying and addressing practical issues in image-prompting techniques. The "conflict-free guidance" is quite novel and applicable to future image prompt studies. The comprehensive experimental validation and open-source code add significant value. While the core method builds upon existing techniques and the impact is incremental, the problems the paper addresses are relevant, and the solutions are effective and well-supported. The paper also provides a more detailed understanding of how to utilize image prompts in a diffusion model.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating Position Bias in Large Language Model Recommendations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02020v1)
- **Authors**: Ethan Bito, Yongli Ren, Estrid He
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are being increasingly explored as general-purpose tools for recommendation tasks, enabling zero-shot and instruction-following capabilities without the need for task-specific training. While the research community is enthusiastically embracing LLMs, there are important caveats to directly adapting them for recommendation tasks. In this paper, we show that LLM-based recommendation models suffer from position bias, where the order of candidate items in a prompt can disproportionately influence the recommendations produced by LLMs. First, we analyse the position bias of LLM-based recommendations on real-world datasets, where results uncover systemic biases of LLMs with high sensitivity to input orders. Furthermore, we introduce a new prompting strategy to mitigate the position bias of LLM recommendation models called Ranking via Iterative SElection (RISE). We compare our proposed method against various baselines on key benchmark datasets. Experiment results show that our method reduces sensitivity to input ordering and improves stability without requiring model fine-tuning or post-processing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Evaluating Position Bias in Large Language Model Recommendations" investigates the position bias inherent in LLM-based recommendation systems.  It demonstrates that LLMs are highly sensitive to the order in which candidate items are presented, leading to inconsistent recommendations when the input order is altered. To mitigate this, the authors propose a novel prompting strategy called Ranking via Iterative SElection (RISE), which breaks down the ranking task into smaller, iterative selections. The paper compares RISE against standard prompting and bootstrapping baselines on MovieLens and Amazon Books datasets, showing that RISE reduces position bias while maintaining competitive ranking performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic investigation of position bias specifically within the context of LLM-based recommendation systems. While position bias is a known phenomenon in traditional recommender systems, its impact and characteristics within LLMs haven't been as rigorously studied. The RISE prompting strategy is a novel approach to address this issue, offering a pragmatic solution that doesn't require model fine-tuning.  The use of Kendall's Tau is a well known metric to perform the analysis. The paper clearly shows that standard prompting, although practical, leads to large position bias. While related work on iterative recommendations do exist, this paper brings it into LLMs and thus it does have novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they highlight a critical challenge in directly applying LLMs to recommendation tasks. The sensitivity to input order can severely limit the reliability and fairness of LLM recommendations. By quantifying this bias and proposing a mitigation strategy, the paper makes a practical contribution to the field. RISE offers a readily implementable approach that can improve the stability and trustworthiness of LLM recommendation models.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of position bias in LLM recommendations.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed, using standard datasets and appropriate evaluation metrics.
    *   **Effective Solution:** RISE demonstrably reduces position bias compared to baselines.
    *   **Practicality:** RISE is a prompting-based approach, making it easy to implement without requiring model fine-tuning.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Datasets:** While MovieLens and Amazon Books are common datasets, testing RISE on a wider range of datasets with different characteristics would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Ablation studies**: It would be good to provide results about the impact of the temperature setting within the LLM.
    *   **Parameter Tuning of RISE:** While the paper touches upon RISE@N, a more systematic analysis of how different N values affect performance would be valuable. The parameter of `temperature` is also missing from the evaluation.
    *   **Lack of comparison with other bias mitigation techniques**: The authors do not compare their proposed method with existing bias mitigation techniques which would have been beneficial to add.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by highlighting and addressing a significant challenge in using LLMs for recommendations. It provides a practical, easy-to-implement solution (RISE) that shows promise.  However, the limited scope of dataset and potential areas of exploration mentioned above limit the overall impact.
