While knowledge distillation isn't entirely new, its *application to aligning attention patterns in RMs* using an interaction-based teacher model is a valuable addition. The *explicit focus on inter-sequence attention* as a critical missing component is also novel. The specific attentional alignment objective is tailored to this problem, which distinguishes it from general KD approaches.

**Significance:** The significance of this work hinges on the centrality of RMs in RLHF. If "attention hacking" is indeed a major contributing factor to instability and poor generalization, as the authors claim, then this work offers a promising avenue for improvement.  The empirical results support their claim, showing performance gains over existing methods and demonstrating better out-of-distribution generalization. The fact that this approach *doesn't introduce inference costs* is a significant practical advantage.  Furthermore, the paper highlights a *fundamental limitation of current RM architectures* - potentially reshaping future RM designs. However, the improvement, while present, is incremental.  More importantly, while the paper makes a good point on the importance of token-level interaction in reward modeling, *the use of BERT (an encoder-only Transformer) as a 'superior' teacher is questionable.*  BERT models can capture bidirectional context within a single sequence but do not model cross-sequence dependencies or 'preferences' inherently. The design choice of choosing an encoder-only teacher model *weakens the key argument* concerning the importance of inter-sequence attention, which is crucial to address the problem of attention hacking.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Identification:** Clearly articulates a significant issue (attention hacking) affecting RMs.
*   **Technical Approach:** Proposes a technically sound and relatively simple solution (interaction distillation) that is easy to implement and doesn't introduce inference overhead.
*   **Empirical Validation:** Provides strong empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of the approach across various tasks (RLHF and OOD).
*   **Clear writing and organization.** The paper is well-written and clearly organized, making it easy to understand the problem and the proposed solution.
*   **Addresses fundamental limitations:** The paper tackles a fundamental architectural limitation rather than merely addressing dataset biases.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Teacher model Selection:** The design choice of the teacher model (BERT) weakens the core claim. The selection of the teacher model is not well motivated and should be a potential avenue for future work.

*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the performance gains are consistent, they aren't groundbreaking. *The gains are more incremental improvements on top of SOTA, with not enough analysis as to why the teacher model's design is better.* More comparisons against other related knowledge distillation methods will make a stronger case.

*   **Limited Ablation:** There's room for more in-depth ablation studies to fully understand the contribution of each component of interaction distillation. In particular, the analysis of why encoder-only teacher model provides better learning signals over the decoder-only reward model is weak.

*   **Generalizability of OOD results**: OOD results are performed on world model and reward benchmarks. There should be more discussion on how these benchmarks can measure the generalizability for RLHF performance.

*   **Lack of comparison to other KD methods**: The paper mentions that previous works for knowledge distillation have focused on output-based imitation. There is no comparison in this study for the performance of attention-based distillation over output-based imitation with other methods on various metrics.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.  The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to addressing a significant problem in RLHF. The empirical results are strong, and the method is practical. However, the incremental nature of the performance gains, the somewhat questionable design choice of the teacher model (encoder-only transformer), and the limited ablation studies hold it back from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. More rigorous justifications are needed to demonstrate the necessity of the technique for improving RLHF and OOD performance.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### HyCodePolicy: Hybrid Language Controllers for Multimodal Monitoring and Decision in Embodied Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02629v1)
- **Authors**: Yibin Liu, Zhixuan Liang, Zanxin Chen, Tianxing Chen, Mengkang Hu, Wanxi Dong, Congsheng Xu, Zhaoming Han, Yusen Qin, Yao Mu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have enabled richer perceptual grounding for code policy generation in embodied agents. However, most existing systems lack effective mechanisms to adaptively monitor policy execution and repair codes during task completion. In this work, we introduce HyCodePolicy, a hybrid language-based control framework that systematically integrates code synthesis, geometric grounding, perceptual monitoring, and iterative repair into a closed-loop programming cycle for embodied agents. Technically, given a natural language instruction, our system first decomposes it into subgoals and generates an initial executable program grounded in object-centric geometric primitives. The program is then executed in simulation, while a vision-language model (VLM) observes selected checkpoints to detect and localize execution failures and infer failure reasons. By fusing structured execution traces capturing program-level events with VLM-based perceptual feedback, HyCodePolicy infers failure causes and repairs programs. This hybrid dual feedback mechanism enables self-correcting program synthesis with minimal human supervision. Our results demonstrate that HyCodePolicy significantly improves the robustness and sample efficiency of robot manipulation policies, offering a scalable strategy for integrating multimodal reasoning into autonomous decision-making pipelines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the HyCodePolicy paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HyCodePolicy, a closed-loop, hybrid language-based control framework designed to enhance the robustness and efficiency of robot manipulation tasks. It leverages large language models (MLLMs) for code synthesis, integrates geometry-aware grounding, incorporates perceptual monitoring using vision-language models (VLMs), and applies targeted code repair based on both symbolic execution logs and VLM-derived feedback. The key innovation is the dual feedback mechanism that facilitates self-correcting program synthesis with minimal human intervention.  Experiments on the RoboTwin platform demonstrate improved task success rates and reduced convergence iterations compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the *integration* of existing components (MLLM-based code generation, symbolic logs, VLM for perception) into a cohesive, closed-loop system. While individual elements like code-as-policy and VLM-based perception exist, the specific *combination* to enable robust self-correction in robotic manipulation is relatively novel. The hybrid feedback loop combining symbolic execution traces with VLM observations is particularly interesting and constitutes a key contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant limitation in current LLM-based robotic control – the lack of robust error handling and adaptation. By closing the loop with perceptual monitoring and targeted code repair, HyCodePolicy moves beyond the one-shot generation paradigm, potentially leading to more reliable and autonomous robotic systems. The increased robustness and sample efficiency demonstrated in the experiments are practically significant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Framework:** The HyCodePolicy framework is clearly articulated with distinct components and a well-defined workflow.
    *   **Hybrid Feedback:** The core contribution - hybrid feedback mechanism utilizing symbolic and perceptual cues for causal repair - is well-motivated and appears effective.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** Experiments on RoboTwin provide evidence for the efficacy of the approach in improving success rates and efficiency. The ablation studies also help to isolate the contributions of individual components.
    *   **Bi2Code Interface:** The redesigned Bi2Code interface shows effort towards modularity and effective deployment of multimodal tracing.
    *   **Addresses a Practical Problem:** The paper tackles a known bottleneck in LLM-based robotics: the need for robust error handling.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Simulation:** The experiments are conducted entirely in simulation. While RoboTwin offers a degree of realism, the performance in real-world scenarios might differ due to factors like sensor noise, calibration inaccuracies, and unmodeled dynamics. A real-world validation would significantly strengthen the paper.
    *   **Task Specific Limitations:** As identified by the authors, handling non-rigid objects, complex articulation, and precise parameter tuning within the existing framework remain significant challenges. The reliance on a predefined API can also limit the scope of tasks. The method struggles with "press", "scan", "shake", "pull", and "pour" skills.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** While comparisons against Code-as-Policies and CodeAct are provided, a broader comparison against other recent closed-loop learning methods for robotics might be beneficial.
    *   **Scalability of VLM Observations:** While effective, the constant VLM observation may not scale efficiently to very long and complicated tasks, requiring further optimization.
    *   **Code Repair Process:** A deeper explanation on exactly how the code is changed and how the new code is generated is required.

*   **Potential Influence:** HyCodePolicy offers a promising direction for incorporating multi-modal reasoning into autonomous decision-making pipelines. It could influence the development of more resilient and adaptable robotic systems, particularly in complex and unstructured environments. The framework also highlights the importance of structured program representation and internal interfaces when using LLMs for robotics.

*   **Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by integrating existing techniques into a novel framework for self-correcting robotic manipulation. However, the reliance on simulation, the task-specific limitations, and the relatively narrow scope of comparison temper its overall impact. The approach holds promise, but requires further validation in real-world scenarios and improvements in its ability to handle more complex robotic tasks.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pointer: Linear-Complexity Long-Range Modeling without Pre-training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02631v1)
- **Authors**: Zixi Li
- **Abstract**: We introduce Pointer, a novel architecture that achieves linear $O(NK)$ complexity for long-range sequence modeling while maintaining superior performance without requiring pre-training. Unlike standard attention mechanisms that compute $O(N^2)$ pairwise interactions, our approach uses layer-wise pointer chaining where each layer's pointer selection depends on previous layer's pointer positions, creating explicit long-distance connections through pointer chains. We demonstrate that this architecture achieves $2$--$10\times$ speedup on long sequences compared to standard transformers, maintains $>95\%$ accuracy on copy tasks at distances up to 2048 tokens, and learns interpretable pointer patterns that reveal structured dependency modeling. Our experiments on efficiency benchmarks, long-range dependency tasks, and interpretability analysis show that Pointer offers a compelling alternative to attention mechanisms for scenarios requiring efficient long-range modeling without pre-training dependencies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Pointer, a novel architecture for long-range sequence modeling designed to overcome the quadratic complexity bottleneck of standard attention mechanisms in transformers.  Pointer achieves linear complexity by replacing dense attention matrices with layer-wise pointer chaining. Each position selects a target position per layer, building structured dependency paths.  The paper demonstrates that Pointer achieves significant speedups compared to standard transformers, maintains high accuracy on copy tasks, and learns interpretable pointer patterns revealing structured dependency modeling.  The architecture requires no pre-training and demonstrates explicit long-range modeling capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using explicit pointer chains for long-range dependency modeling is potentially novel. The shift from dense attention matrices to explicit, learnable pointers is a significant departure from standard transformer architectures. However, the concept of pointer networks exists; the novelty lies in applying it hierarchically within a transformer-like architecture for direct dependency modeling rather than output prediction. The key lies in the layer-wise pointer chaining, which dynamically creates structured patterns. Whether or not this constitutes a groundbreaking advancement, rather than a refinement of existing ideas is debatable.

* **Significance:** The paper's potential significance stems from its ability to address a critical limitation of transformers: the quadratic complexity of attention. By achieving linear complexity, Pointer could enable the efficient processing of much longer sequences, opening up new possibilities in areas like document summarization, long-form question answering, and genomic sequence analysis. The elimination of pre-training is another significant advantage, making it easier to apply to new domains with limited data. The claimed interpretability is also important if it can be reliably demonstrated in practice.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Linear Complexity:** The O(NK) complexity claim, if substantiated, is a major advantage. The experimental results do show evidence of this efficiency boost.
    * **No Pre-training:** The ability to learn from scratch is a substantial benefit, as it reduces dependence on large pre-trained models.
    * **Interpretability:** The promise of interpretable pointer patterns is compelling, offering a way to understand how the model is reasoning about long-range dependencies. The initial visualizations support this.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides experimental results on efficiency benchmarks and long-range dependency tasks, demonstrating the potential of the architecture.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Baseline Comparisons:** The lack of comprehensive comparisons to other efficient transformer variants (e.g., Longformer, Reformer) is a weakness. The paper mentions implementation difficulties, but this significantly limits the ability to assess Pointer's true performance relative to the state-of-the-art.  This is particularly problematic as the paper is explicitly positioning itself as an alternative to these architectures.
    * **Hardware Limitation:** The hardware limitation could influence the results as modern GPUs are known for efficient matrix computations.
    * **Copy Task Simplicity:** The copy task is a somewhat simplistic evaluation.  While useful, it doesn't fully capture the nuances of real-world language modeling or reasoning tasks.
    * **Interpretability Claims Need Stronger Support:**  The interpretability analysis, while promising, is still preliminary.  More rigorous quantitative measures and analysis are needed to fully validate the claims about the model's ability to learn meaningful dependency patterns.  It's important to consider if the interpretability actually leads to better understanding of the model or whether the explanation is simply "post-hoc" which doesn't improve understanding of the system.
    * **Reliance on Specific Hardware:** It is crucial to verify the performance and scaling on other hardware, particularly in more standard distributed training setups, as these can expose performance bottlenecks not apparent on a single machine.

* **Potential Impact:** If Pointer can overcome its current limitations, it could have a significant impact on the field by enabling the efficient and interpretable modeling of long sequences.  However, its ultimate impact will depend on its ability to outperform or complement existing approaches in real-world applications.  The impact is also contingent on the architecture scaling up to larger models and datasets.

* **Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a potentially novel architecture with promising results in terms of efficiency and interpretability.  The key strengths are the demonstrated linear complexity and the ability to learn without pre-training.  However, the limited experimental validation, particularly the lack of comprehensive comparisons to other efficient transformer variants, and the relatively simplistic evaluation tasks prevent a higher score. The reliance on a particular hardware setup also creates questions about general applicability.  The interpretability claims are also intriguing, but they need more rigorous support. The paper offers a good starting point, but substantial further research is needed to fully realize its potential and justify a higher valuation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Test Set Quality in Multilingual LLM Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02635v1)
- **Authors**: Kranti Chalamalasetti, Gabriel Bernier-Colborne, Yvan Gauthier, Sowmya Vajjala
- **Abstract**: Several multilingual benchmark datasets have been developed in a semi-automatic manner in the recent past to measure progress and understand the state-of-the-art in the multilingual capabilities of Large Language Models. However, there is not a lot of attention paid to the quality of the datasets themselves, despite the existence of previous work in identifying errors in even fully human-annotated test sets. In this paper, we manually analyze recent multilingual evaluation sets in two languages - French and Telugu, identifying several errors in the process. We compare the performance difference across several LLMs with the original and revised versions of the datasets and identify large differences (almost 10% in some cases) in both languages). Based on these results, we argue that test sets should not be considered immutable and should be revisited, checked for correctness, and potentially versioned. We end with some recommendations for both the dataset creators as well as consumers on addressing the dataset quality issues.
- **Summary**: This paper investigates the quality of multilingual evaluation datasets for Large Language Models (LLMs), focusing on French and Telugu versions of the INCLUDE44 dataset and replicating the analysis on a Telugu subset of MILU. The authors manually analyze these datasets, identifying issues such as unanswerable questions, incorrect question/answer pairs, and language inconsistencies. They then compare the performance of various LLMs on the original and cleaned versions of the datasets, revealing significant performance differences (up to 10% accuracy change in some cases) between the two versions in both languages. The authors advocate for a more critical approach to test set quality, emphasizing the need for continuous quality assurance, versioning, and qualitative analysis. They also suggest further research into automatic methods for identifying dataset quality issues.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the paper doesn't introduce entirely new techniques or groundbreaking findings, it makes a valuable contribution by highlighting a critical, often overlooked aspect of multilingual LLM evaluation: dataset quality. The work's novelty lies in its empirical investigation of specific multilingual datasets and its quantification of the impact of data quality issues on LLM performance. The manual error analysis and the comparison of LLM performance on original and cleaned datasets contribute new insights into the challenges of creating robust multilingual benchmarks.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to influence how researchers and practitioners approach multilingual LLM evaluation. By demonstrating the tangible impact of dataset quality on LLM performance, the authors raise awareness of the need for more rigorous data curation practices. The call for versioning and continuous quality assurance of test sets could lead to more reliable and reproducible evaluation results. The paper provides a practical demonstration and highlights concrete areas of improvement in multilingual benchmark dataset creation. However, the scope is limited by focusing on a relatively small number of languages and datasets, and the annotation approach is somewhat basic.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear and concise analysis of data quality issues in multilingual evaluation datasets.
    *   The empirical evaluation demonstrates the significant impact of data quality on LLM performance.
    *   The authors offer practical recommendations for improving dataset creation and evaluation practices.
    *   The replication study with the MILU dataset provides further evidence of the generalizability of the findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The analysis focuses on only two languages (French and Telugu) which limits the generalizability of the findings. Although the authors mention other languages, the bulk of their analysis lies within these two languages.
    *   The annotation process, while straightforward, is also somewhat basic. A more detailed or structured annotation approach could have provided richer insights into the types of errors and their potential impact.
    *   The paper does not explore automatic or semi-automatic methods for identifying data quality issues in detail, instead mentioning this as future work.
    *   The number of datasets analyzed is limited. Expanding the evaluation to include more multilingual benchmark datasets would strengthen the claims.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the community by:
    *   Raising awareness of the importance of data quality in multilingual LLM evaluation.
    *   Encouraging more rigorous data curation practices for multilingual benchmarks.
    *   Motivating further research into automatic methods for identifying and correcting data quality issues.
    *   Leading to the adoption of versioning and continuous quality assurance practices for multilingual evaluation datasets.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a sound, well-executed empirical study that addresses a relevant and important issue in the field of multilingual LLM evaluation. While the findings are not revolutionary, the work provides valuable insights and practical recommendations that can improve the reliability and reproducibility of evaluation results. The limitations regarding the scope and annotation approach are adequately acknowledged. Given these strengths and weaknesses, a score of 6 is appropriate. The paper makes a solid contribution, though its impact is somewhat limited by the scope of the analysis and the simplicity of the methods used.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evaluating Variance in Visual Question Answering Benchmarks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02645v1)
- **Authors**: Nikitha SR
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for visual question answering (VQA), enabling reasoning and contextual understanding across visual and textual modalities. Despite their advancements, the evaluation of MLLMs on VQA benchmarks often relies on point estimates, overlooking the significant variance in performance caused by factors such as stochastic model outputs, training seed sensitivity, and hyperparameter configurations. This paper critically examines these issues by analyzing variance across 14 widely used VQA benchmarks, covering diverse tasks such as visual reasoning, text understanding, and commonsense reasoning. We systematically study the impact of training seed, framework non-determinism, model scale, and extended instruction finetuning on performance variability. Additionally, we explore Cloze-style evaluation as an alternate assessment strategy, studying its effectiveness in reducing stochasticity and improving reliability across benchmarks. Our findings highlight the limitations of current evaluation practices and advocate for variance-aware methodologies to foster more robust and reliable development of MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Evaluating Variance in Visual Question Answering Benchmarks" critically examines the evaluation practices of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) on Visual Question Answering (VQA) benchmarks. It highlights the common practice of relying on point estimates for performance, which overlooks significant variance stemming from factors like stochastic model outputs, training seed sensitivity, and hyperparameter configurations. The authors analyze variance across 14 VQA benchmarks, covering various tasks like visual reasoning, text understanding, and commonsense reasoning.  They investigate the impact of training seed, framework non-determinism, model scale, and instruction fine-tuning on performance variability. The paper also explores Cloze-style evaluation as an alternative strategy to reduce stochasticity. The findings underscore the limitations of current evaluation methodologies and advocate for variance-aware approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of assessing the *variance* in VQA benchmarks, rather than simply reporting point estimates, is a valuable and under-explored perspective. While individual factors like seed sensitivity are known anecdotally, a systematic analysis across a diverse range of benchmarks is a significant contribution. The investigation of Cloze-style evaluation is not entirely novel, but its application and analysis specifically in the context of MLLM variance is relevant.

*   **Significance:** The findings are highly significant for the VQA and broader MLLM research community. The paper convincingly demonstrates that reported performance differences on benchmarks can be misleading due to substantial variance. This has direct implications for how researchers interpret and compare models. The call for variance-aware evaluation can lead to more robust and reliable model development.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The evaluation across 14 benchmarks provides a strong and diverse dataset for analysis.
    *   **Systematic Investigation:**  The authors systematically investigate multiple factors contributing to variance.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:** The paper provides a clear call to action for incorporating variance-aware methods in future research.
    *   **Rigorous experimentation:** Conducted five independent runs and compared results
    *   **Good choice of models:** Used LLaVA and InternVL for their experiments and trained these models which makes the experiments reproducible

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Exploration of Causes:** While the paper identifies sources of variance, it doesn't deeply investigate the underlying *reasons* *why* certain benchmarks are more susceptible. This limits the ability to prescribe specific solutions beyond general variance-aware practices.
    *   **Dependency on Choice of Benchmarks:** The conclusions are inherently tied to the chosen benchmarks.  A different set of tasks could lead to different observations about which factors are most important.
    *   **Scope of Solution:** Cloze evaluation is one potential path to address variance, but its significant reduction in accuracy on many benchmarks makes it less appealing as a default. The paper doesn't explore a wide range of potential mitigation strategies.
    *   **Limited ablation experiments:** Could have experimented with different hyperparameters to better understand the variations caused by hyperparameter tuning

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to change how VQA benchmarks are used and interpreted, leading to more careful and robust model development. The discussion on the limitations of current benchmarks and the need for variance-aware methods will likely influence future research directions.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper provides a valuable and necessary correction to current practices in VQA model evaluation. It highlights an important issue and presents compelling evidence to support its claims. While the analysis could be deeper in identifying the *causes* of variance and a wider range of mitigation strategies could be explored, the paper's contribution is significant enough to warrant a high score. This paper should influence standard practice in the VQA research community.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LOST: Low-rank and Sparse Pre-training for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02668v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaxi Li, Lu Yin, Li Shen, Jinjin Xu, Liwu Xu, Tianjin Huang, Wenwu Wang, Shiwei Liu, Xilu Wang
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance across a wide range of tasks, their massive scale incurs prohibitive computational and memory costs for pre-training from scratch. Recent studies have investigated the use of low-rank parameterization as a means of reducing model size and training cost. In this context, sparsity is often employed as a complementary technique to recover important information lost in low-rank compression by capturing salient features in the residual space. However, existing approaches typically combine low-rank and sparse components in a simplistic or ad hoc manner, often resulting in undesirable performance degradation compared to full-rank training. In this paper, we propose \textbf{LO}w-rank and \textbf{S}parse pre-\textbf{T}raining (\textbf{LOST}) for LLMs, a novel method that ingeniously integrates low-rank and sparse structures to enable effective training of LLMs from scratch under strict efficiency constraints. LOST applies singular value decomposition to weight matrices, preserving the dominant low-rank components, while allocating the remaining singular values to construct channel-wise sparse components to complement the expressiveness of low-rank training. We evaluate LOST on LLM pretraining ranging from 60M to 7B parameters. Our experiments show that LOST achieves competitive or superior performance compared to full-rank models, while significantly reducing both memory and compute overhead. Moreover, Code is available at \href{https://github.com/JiaxiLi1/LOST-Low-rank-and-Sparse-Training-for-Large-Language-Models}{LOST Repo}
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LOST: Low-rank and Sparse Pre-training for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LOST (Low-rank and Sparse pre-Training), a novel method for pre-training large language models (LLMs) from scratch with limited computational resources. LOST cleverly combines low-rank approximation and channel-wise structured sparsity. It uses Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) to identify and preserve the dominant low-rank components of weight matrices.  The remaining singular values are used to construct a channel-wise sparse component, complementing the expressiveness of the low-rank training. The paper demonstrates that LOST achieves comparable or superior performance to full-rank models and other existing low-rank pre-training methods, while significantly reducing memory and computational costs, particularly in the LLaMA architecture. The authors present extensive experimental results, including pre-training from scratch and fine-tuning on various tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating low-rank and structured sparsity isn't entirely new, as other works, notably SLTrain, have explored similar directions. The novelty lies in the *specific co-design strategy* employed by LOST.  Instead of simply combining separately initialized low-rank and sparse components, LOST leverages SVD to ensure that the sparse component captures information orthogonal to the low-rank subspace, leading to better expressiveness. The channel-wise structured sparsity is also significant, enabling hardware acceleration and efficient memory access patterns. Also the usage of SVD on full-rank initialization to guide this process of construction low-rank and sparse matrix is an interesting idea to explore.

*   **Significance:** Reducing the pre-training cost of LLMs is a very important problem. If this can be achieved with minimal performance degradation then it allows wider access and democratization of LLM training.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Effective co-design:** The key strength is the SVD-guided decomposition, which enables the low-rank and sparse components to complement each other.
    *   **Channel-wise Sparsity:**  The structured sparsity improves memory efficiency compared to element-wise sparsity, which is crucial for practical LLM training.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation on multiple model sizes and tasks, demonstrating consistent improvements over baselines. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the design choices and the importance of different components.
    *   **Scalability (to some extent):** The evaluation included models up to 7B parameters, showcasing some degree of scalability.
    *   **Fine-tuning performance:** Performance also measured in fine tuning which is also an imporant factor.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scale Evaluation:** The main limitation is the absence of evaluation on significantly larger models (e.g., exceeding 10B parameters). While the 7B results are promising, it's unclear if the method scales effectively to the sizes used in state-of-the-art LLMs. This is acknowledged in the paper.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:**  LLM training is generally very sensitive to hyperparameter tuning. While the paper explores the impact of some hyperparameters, it's unclear how robust LOST is to variations in other parameters, and whether the presented configurations generalize well to different datasets or architectures. The dependence of performance on the relative weighting factor (gamma) also needs to be looked into more.
    *   **Computational Resources:** Many details regarding how the computational resources were handled (like GPUs) are not made transparent.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on efficient LLM pre-training by providing a practical and effective method for reducing computational costs. If the method scales well to larger models, it could enable broader access to LLM pre-training for researchers and organizations with limited resources.

*   **Overall:** This is a solid paper with a well-motivated method and promising results. The main weakness is the lack of evaluation at very large scales. The co-design strategy and channel-wise sparsity are key strengths. I think the claim that it beats 'full-rank' models is a bit inflated, the improvement margins are within range.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the novelty and significance of the method in the context of efficient LLM pre-training. While there are some limitations, the overall approach is well-designed and validated with sufficient experiments, making it a worthwhile contribution. The co-design of sparsity and rank selection is a meaningful improvement over previous works. The lack of extremely large-scale evaluations prevents a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MedVLThinker: Simple Baselines for Multimodal Medical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02669v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoke Huang, Juncheng Wu, Hui Liu, Xianfeng Tang, Yuyin Zhou
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have introduced a new paradigm in AI by enabling models to ``think before responding" via chain-of-thought reasoning. However, the absence of open and reproducible recipes for building reasoning-centric medical LMMs hinders community-wide research, analysis, and comparison. In this paper, we present MedVLThinker, a suite of simple yet strong baselines. Our fully open recipe consists of: (1) systematic data curation for both text-only and image-text medical data, filtered according to varying levels of reasoning difficulty, and (2) two training paradigms: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on distilled reasoning traces and Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) based on final answer correctness. Across extensive experiments on the Qwen2.5-VL model family (3B, 7B) and six medical QA benchmarks, we find that RLVR consistently and significantly outperforms SFT. Additionally, under the RLVR framework, a key, counter-intuitive finding is that training on our curated text-only reasoning data provides a more substantial performance boost than training on multimodal image-text data. Our best open 7B model, trained using the RLVR recipe on text-only data, establishes a new state-of-the-art on existing public VQA benchmarks, surpassing all previous open-source medical LMMs. Furthermore, scaling our model to 32B achieves performance on par with the proprietary GPT-4o. We release all curated data, models, and code to provide the community with a strong, open foundation for future research in multimodal medical reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MEDVLTHINKER, a suite of simple baselines for multimodal medical reasoning. It addresses the lack of open and reproducible methods for building reasoning-centric medical Large Multimodal Models (LMMs).  The authors provide a fully open recipe involving: 1) systematic data curation of both text-only and image-text medical data, filtered based on reasoning difficulty, and 2) two training paradigms: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on distilled reasoning traces and Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) based on final answer correctness. The study extensively evaluates the approach using Qwen2.5-VL models and six medical QA benchmarks. Key findings include: RLVR consistently outperforms SFT, text-only training can outperform multimodal training, and the 32B version achieves performance on par with the proprietary GPT-40. The authors release all data, models, and code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Openness and Reproducibility:** The most significant strength is the fully open-source nature of the work. Releasing the data curation process, model code, and trained models is crucial for advancing research in this area. This contrasts with many existing works that are either closed-source or only release model weights.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive and systematic evaluation across multiple models, training paradigms, and datasets. This allows for a clear comparison of different approaches and provides valuable insights into their effectiveness.
    *   **Key Findings:** The counter-intuitive finding that text-only training can outperform multimodal training is significant. This suggests that data quality and the focus on reasoning are crucial, potentially even more so than directly incorporating visual information. The success of RLVR is also an important contribution, highlighting the potential of learning reasoning from answer correctness alone. The 32B result matching GPT-4 level raises the bar for open medical reasoning models.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper investigates the impact of training data, training paradigms, and model scale, providing a holistic understanding of the factors that influence performance. The difficulty-based filtering approach is a good step towards curriculum learning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Data Quality of Image-Text Data:** The paper readily acknowledges the limitations of the image-text training data (PMC-VQA). Relying on GPT-3.5 generated Q&A pairs limits data quality.
    *   **Limited Task Scope:** The focus on single-turn question-answering, while a useful starting point, does not reflect the complexity of real-world clinical scenarios.
    *   **Relatively Short RLVR Training:** While the RLVR results are impressive, the paper notes that only a few epochs were used. It is reasonable to question whether further, more extensive RLVR training could yield significantly larger performance gains
    *   **Static Difficulty Filtering:** The difficulty-based filtering strategy is static, and there is a question of whether the filter parameters were optimal for the full model sizes.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *The contribution of establishing open-source baselines for medical VLMs is notable. There is a critical need in the field for democratized research. The rigorous data curation process is another important aspect. The RLVR result, and matching GPT-4 open source, are additional strengths.
*However, there is limited novelty. The paper simply applies existing RLVR techniques with medical data. The methodology for data curation is simple. Additionally, single-turn QA tasks do not reflect real-world clinical tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The release of MEDVLTHINKER is likely to stimulate further research in this area. The open baselines and code will allow other researchers to build upon this work and develop more advanced methods for multimodal medical reasoning.
    *   The findings on data modality and training paradigms could influence the direction of future research, encouraging a greater focus on data quality and reasoning-centric approaches.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

MEDVLTHINKER is a solid and valuable contribution. However, its strengths are primarily in its open and comprehensive nature rather than methodological innovation. While the results are interesting and potentially influential, the underlying techniques (RLVR, data filtering) are not entirely new. The main strength lies in democratization, providing the community with a much-needed open toolkit. The work is significant as a starting point for future research, the lack of novel methods and restricted task scope limits the contribution. Releasing data and code are important and well above average, but the model training and results are also above average - not revolutionary. It would be 5 but the 32B result is very impressive and open source is very valuable, pushing it to 6.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Raw Data Matters: Enhancing Prompt Tuning by Internal Augmentation on Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02671v1)
- **Authors**: Haoyang Li, Liang Wang, Chao Wang, Siyu Zhou, Jing Jiang, Yan Peng, Guodong Long
- **Abstract**: For CLIP-based prompt tuning, introducing more data as additional knowledge for enhancing fine-tuning process is proved to be an effective approach. Existing data amplification strategies for prompt tuning typically rely on external knowledge (e.g., large language models or pre-structured knowledge bases), resulting in higher costs for data collection and processing, while generally ignoring further utilization of features in image modality. To address this, we propose Augmentation-driven Prompt Tuning (AugPT), a self-contained distillation-based prompt tuning approach using only internal augmentation on raw dataset to better exploit known features. Specifically, AugPT employs self-supervised augmentation on unlabeled images in the training set, and introduces a novel gating mechanism based on consensus test, reusing the pre-trained prompt tuning backbone model to spontaneously filter noisy samples, further enhancing the quality of augmented views. Extensive experiments validate that AugPT simultaneously enhances model performance and generalization capability without using appended external knowledge. The code of AugPT is available at: https://github.com/JREion/AugPT .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Raw Data Matters: Enhancing Prompt Tuning by Internal Augmentation on Vision-Language Models" proposes Augmentation-driven Prompt Tuning (AugPT), a self-contained distillation-based approach for improving CLIP-based prompt tuning. AugPT leverages self-supervised augmentation on unlabeled images within the training set and introduces a novel consensus-based gating mechanism to filter noisy augmented views. By exploiting internal data augmentation instead of relying on external knowledge resources, AugPT aims to enhance model performance and generalization capabilities in a parameter-efficient manner. Experimental results across 11 datasets demonstrate that AugPT achieves state-of-the-art performance without additional knowledge or data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of several techniques. While self-supervised augmentation and knowledge distillation are not individually novel, the *specific way* they are integrated in AugPT, *without external knowledge*, appears to be a unique contribution.  The consensus testing gate is a valuable addition, addressing a key limitation in naive self-supervised augmentation. This is a targeted modification to improve prompt tuning specifically, not just applying existing techniques wholesale. The theoretical explanation of why the consensus test works and improves results helps strengthen the paper.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a practical problem: the scarcity of labeled data in downstream tasks for VLMs. Reducing the reliance on external knowledge and/or extra data collection offers a potential cost-saving advantage. The performance gains reported, especially in few-shot settings, indicate that AugPT could be a valuable tool for adapting VLMs to new tasks efficiently. However, the actual "real-world" savings depend heavily on the cost of running self-supervised augmentation and distillation compared to acquiring external knowledge, which the paper doesn't explicitly compare.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong experimental results across a diverse set of datasets.
    *   Clear explanation of the method and its components.
    *   Addresses a practical problem in VLM fine-tuning.
    *   The "plug-and-play" adaptability (as claimed in main text) demonstrates broad applicablity for AugPT in distillation-based frameworks.
    *   The limitations are acknowledged and discussed, which show the maturity of this work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The "internal" augmentation may still be introducing biases from the *pre-trained* model. Although it avoid external data, biases will remain as long as the pre-trained parameters are used.
    *   While the experimental results compare favorably with models *using external knowledge*, there is no analysis of the *actual cost* (time, compute, etc.) saved.
    *   The improvements, although significant, appear to be relatively incremental in several datasets.
    *   The framework depends heavily on an initial set of "good" prompts, which may require some data anyway and the prompts affect downstream performance.
    *   Lack of specific examples for failure cases to visually understand why AugPT did not perform well in certain scenarios.
    *   The analysis of each individual parameter (ie. strength of augmentation, depth of network) may not be intuitive to most readers and could benefit from extra explanation.

*   **Potential Influence:**  If AugPT can be shown to be genuinely cost-effective in real-world applications, it could influence how VLMs are adapted to new tasks, especially in resource-constrained environments.  The idea of exploiting *internal* data augmentation for prompt tuning could inspire further research in this direction, potentially leading to even more efficient and effective fine-tuning methods.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

While the component techniques are individually known, the paper presents a well-executed and novel *integration* that demonstrably improves performance in a practical setting. The consensus testing gate adds significant value. The clear presentation and strong empirical results support the claims. However, the lack of a real-world cost analysis and the somewhat incremental improvements in several datasets prevent a higher score.  The reliance on pre-trained models and "good" prompts also limits its overall novelty and impact. Nevertheless, the work provides a valuable contribution to the field of VLM adaptation, and it should stimulate further research on the topic.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10
